ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e2637	Mr Federal Minister, I ask for the answer.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e2654	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to answer Mr Reichhold's question as to whether our calves' sisters are harmed by the fixed slaughter weight of 82 kilograms, by a clear no, and that is also justified. The establishment of maximum weight limits for these new support measures was aimed at creating a global and EU-wide release of the calf and cattle market. In that sense, it was necessary that in everyone – in everyone! – Member State had to reduce the slaughter weights of the calves. Although an EU-wide average weight would have meant a higher slaughter weight for Austria, it would have failed to achieve the above-mentioned objective of market relief. They also know that the background to this early marketing premium for calves is to provide a quantitative discharge of the beef market on the one hand and, on the other hand, to find a new market opportunity for classical veal. I emphasize in particular that we have succeeded in including female calves in this early marketing premium – for the first time in the history of the EU Rinder Market Regulation, there is a measure for female animals.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e5533	Mr Federal Minister, my question is: [[/PAKT/VHG/XX/M/M_00060/index.shtml -- 60/M]] What results have been achieved so far through the mandatory evaluation of the environmental impact of the OPUL environmental programme provided for in Regulation 2078/92/EC?
ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e5560	Please have the answer, Minister of State.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e5577	Deputies! The 1995 Environmental Assessment Report of Public Health Measures is now in the final version. ==References== In November, two days ago, this large working group took the decision that this final report is now continuing after Brussels. This final report on the eco-effects of the environmental programme is divided according to the objectives of protection – soil protection, water protection, for example – and I can tell you that this evaluation report is an absolute success report for this environmental programme. Some 180 000 farms took part in the measures in 1995, taking into account more than 90 percent of the agricultural area. On about two thirds of the arable land, the fruit sequence is made more diverse by entering into the obligation not to have more than 75 percent of cereals and maize or to support intermediate fruit cultivation through the corresponding greening measure – one of the most effective measures in the protection of groundwater and erosion. Extensive production, without expecting the alpine areas, has been converted to about 10 percent of the agricultural land by organic farming. We have, for example, brought additional land into this more extensive form of production, so that about 35 per cent of the agricultural area can be expected to be extensively managed. We also have evidence of the decline in income-enhancing operating resources. For example, nitrogen use fell by 8 percent, phosphorus use by 12 percent, potassium use by 20 percent. These declines – I point out – have already been added to a very low level, because we had very little use in international comparison before ÖPUL. The use of plant protection products fell by 6 percent, that of insecticides and fungicides by 10 percent each. The decrease in growth stabilisers is 58 percent. There is also a gratifying upsurge in the use of beneficial organisms. On about 21,000 hectares these are already used as a substitute for chemical plant protection products. Extensive animal husbandry has also achieved positive impetus through these programmes, i.e. the increase in the number of cows in breeding and breeding by 120,000, compared to 1994 by 133 percent. Alternative animal husbandry, for example in the goat sector, also increased by 7 percent to 8 percent. I can therefore say today that this final report on the environmental effects of the environmental programme - as has already been said - is a real success report and we can say good conscience in Brussels – and wherever we need this statement: The environmental programme has achieved its objectives.

And you also know that there are currently support measures in the European Union which do not take account of these questions – I would say – because in the European Union, too, almost all the Member States, as I have already said, recognise that agriculture and the environment are inseparable. One must be united, including in the sense of sustainable development of agriculture, and I therefore assume that environmental criteria are increasingly being introduced as a condition for support in Europe in the case of support measures.

[[Intrusion of the Abg. Mother.]] – Please don't hesitate, ladies and gentlemen! [[New call of the abg. Mother.]] That will not be the case, quite the contrary! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] [[Further call of the Abg. Mother.]] I expect, however, that constructive cooperation, which has been exercised in particular by the main social security body, will continue so that we can clarify the technical details, the financial needs and the data protection requirements, ladies and gentlemen. The crucial point of the law we are dealing with today is structural reform in the area of the financing of hospitals. This is a huge structural reform. The responsibility for public hospitals and private non-profit hospitals, which were previously included in the KRAZAF, is transferred to the countries. In accordance with our principle of subsidiarity, the countries have sole responsibility for hospitals, both in financial matters and in other areas. In 1997 to 2000, they receive an amount which is precisely defined. This is set out in Article 15a, which we will still decide on today. There are nine national funds out of which the hospitals are 1st. January 1997 and there is the performance-oriented financing and settlement system. In other words, the benefits of the hospitals will be paid according to performance criteria and no longer flat-rate after care days, as is often the case to this day. They know that in some federal states, especially in Vorarlberg, this performance-oriented settlement system was already practised in 1996. What does that mean? – First of all, it means a clear cost limitation for health insurance companies. The costs for health insurance companies can no longer be recovered. This has also created clarity for the insured, as health insurance companies must be financed by contributions from the insured. Secondly, We have achieved a real federalisation of the funding of hospitals. This is very important, and I would like to stress this particularly. Ladies and gentlemen! We have thus achieved a service-friendly health care fund, and we have also found a fair solution for those private hospitals that have not been included in the KRAZAF. This fair solution clearly determines the financial volume provided to them. Although the main association will continue to conclude contracts with private hospitals, there is also a clear cost limitation in this area. The same is true of accident insurance hospitals. In summary, this means: In the future, we have nine national funds for the financing of hospitals, we have another fund for private hospitals, and we have another, the eleventh, fund for accident insurance hospitals. This is a clear, permanent funding of hospitals – an extremely important measure. I think this is a fundamental structural reform, a fundamental change in the direction of federalisation, self-employment, personal responsibility at hospitals, countries and health insurance institutions. Another very important concern for us is that we set an emphasis on the long-term unemployed today. We want long-term unemployed to get a job! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] This can only be achieved if we say that we are using the financial resources normally provided as emergency aid as active labour market support. We make these financial resources available to those employers who employ such long-term unemployed people who would otherwise not be able to get a job. Passive labour market promotion funds are made available for active labour market policies so that people get a job. It only makes sense to give them support if they can also work. That's our goal. [[President Dr. Fischer shall take over the chairmanship.]] I would like to thank the Minister for giving this impetus and for initiating this support or this new initiative to this extent. I would like to admit quite openly: The initiative came from the Minister for Social Affairs and I would like to thank him for it. However, we take them along and we expect the necessary accents to be set too. In this sense, I believe it is groundbreaking changes that we are deciding on today, and we should all give our support to these changes.

This does not mean, however, that if more than 200 000 unemployed people in the country are present, they can be satisfied with these figures. Considering labour market policy in the European context, a total of 2,430 billion schilling were spent on labour market policy in the EU – without Italy – in 1995. This amount corresponds almost exactly to the gross domestic product that Austria generates in a year with 2 500 billion schilling. To spend about two thirds of them on so-called passive measures, as for the financial support of the unemployed, this appears too much compared to active labour market policies, for which only around 850 billion schilling are spent. We understand active labour market policies as job creation programmes, retrainings, job placements or employer promotions. In Austria and Spain – which has reported "profile" now – the money is used mainly – under quotation marks – for "stampling". In Sweden, on the other hand, more than half, 54 percent, are invested in the active struggle for more jobs. The prerequisite for this is: If access to the labour market cannot be achieved without the granting of special inclusion aid to the employer, the labour market service may benefit from unemployment insurance. If we look at the development of long-term unemployment in parallel with the continuing rise in unemployment, we must say that since the beginning of the 1980s, there has been an increasing concentration of unemployment burden on those groups who are unable to get back into the employment system in a short time. Of the persons affected by unemployment in 1995, a total of 687 000, 24 percent were unemployed for more than six months. As in recent years, it has also been shown in 1995 that age is a certain measure of the risk of long-term unemployment. This is evident in the proportion of long-term unemployed in each age group. I can't save you a few numbers. This survey shows that the proportion of long-term unemployed is constantly increasing, from 50 onwards. Year of life is rising enormously. Regionally specific, the problem is concentrated on some federal states: Lower Austria, Styria, Vienna. This is related to industry-specific priorities such as metal, trade, storage and the like. And as a starting point for long-term unemployment, there are primarily reduced reemployment opportunities which result from the employment system and supply competition in the labour market. In particular, high proportions of long-term unemployed are observed in sectors with declining employment. Even in 1996, the problem of long-term unemployment has not been eroded, and the existing data confirm that the reemployment prospects of long-term unemployed people continue to deteriorate. On average, from January to October 1996, some 68,000 persons were unemployed for more than six months, compared with the previous year, which was 4.3 percent more. This process has been accelerating since spring. What strategies can be developed? In the public and political debate both at national and EU level, the issue of long-term unemployment has been more focused. Preventive and reintegration strategies are central to resource use for labour market policies. For example, the European Structural Funds' interventions focus on the integration of long-term unemployed. This area is also central to the objectives of the Federal Minister: The prevention of long-term unemployment as a result of economic structural change and social change is a human, social and economic necessity. In doing so, the difficult group of problem-employed people requires the continuous development of innovative instruments. In this sense, the Austrian labour market policy, in line with the employment and labour market policies of the European Union, pursues the fundamental objective of activation before supply and mediation before administration. With the current measure of activating passive benefits, the active integration promotion of long-term unemployed persons is provided with an additional instrument. A simple model calculation: The following effects are effected by a national cost-benefit account: In the case of a mediation volume of 5 000 people, there is an effort for the development of labour market policy, amounting to around 305 million schilling. This is compared with wage tax revenue due to the employment of around 154 million schilling and social security contributions of 241 million schilling. The increase in revenue thus amounts to around 90 million schillings, which can be re-entered in this desired process. The AMS determines the granting of special integration aid by directives and these directives are laid down in the law. Ladies and gentlemen! ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e8854	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! [[President Dr. Brauneder shall take over the chairmanship.]] This innovation – by the way, this is a government template, which today needs to be particularly highlighted in such a field – is a very classic case of an excess legislation with the tendency to regulate more than has to be regulated; even more to be regulated than to be regulated. The philosophy of the provisions introduced here is quite surprising, since it must be borne in mind that these are areas of dispositive labour law. The paragraphs which are to be voted today bring about the following paradox: It is initially expressly forbidden to work on two consecutive Saturdays in the afternoon – which is, as it were, the social partnership flank at the threatening shop opening times; "threating" from the point of view of the social partners. – It is therefore banned for the sake of caution. Then exceptions are defined, for example with regard to sales activity on the last four Saturdays before the 24. December. – It can be said that this is interesting, but that is already the case and is now laid down in the law. In other words: The Advent – I ask you to pay attention to this: the Advent – is considered less protective from the law. Now I'm saying, I am of the opinion that what applies to the Advent could actually apply throughout the year, because I do not see why one sees for the Advent, the time of reflection and the A return should be made, labour law is more relaxed, but the reins are strictly attracted for the rest of the year. If one considers protection rules to be useful – although they are completely non-economic and non-animal, I say so – then it is precisely with regard to the Advent that people should have time to be able to devote themselves to the Saturday of reflection and preparation for the following Advent Sunday. – But in the law it is not, but just the other way round. There is another exception to the 1991 opening law, and then the real phenomenon comes: There is the possibility of setting exceptions under operating agreements and collective agreements. I ask you: What kind of legislator do we believe that we do not trust the social partners that, if we allow this to happen on Saturday in principle, they will set restrictions on collective agreements and business agreements? I have also said this at the Committee meeting: Obviously, the social partners no longer have the confidence to come up with a useful working paper, if not previously assured that a page – i.e. one-sided – can say "njet". I mean, in this case, it is a rejection of the employee's representatives. If they do so in the law, they apparently do not trust themselves to achieve a constructive outcome in the way of negotiation. I believe that if the social partners have come to such an extent that the two sides face each other with such great distrust that they need a legal "Njet" regulation, then the system has reached its limits from the mechanics. Clearly, we no longer have any confidence in the chosen works councils, no confidence in the collective contractual partners. This House seems to be of the opinion that it is necessary to press this area into such a narrow legal corset that it is not possible to live autonomy, self-management, self-determination and genuine in-house co-operation, but, in the event of an emergency, everything is strictly regulated and only according to law. [[Congratulations at the Liberal Forum.]] This philosophy irritates me so much, because what would have come out of collective agreements or even in business agreements – and would it have been similar to what we write in the law here – would have been near to life. However, we are going the opposite way, that is, we are forcing a presumptuous arrangement and, for the sake of mercy, allowing something else to be. If this is our answer to the problems of globalisation, our answer to the dynamization of the social partners living in the enterprises, then it is very bad. I'll tell you: People will get away from you. They will no longer feel that you really represent them, but they will feel that there are foreign decisions somewhere about the clouds, but they are affected by them. You'll mean: Whether we've organized it or not, they're not interested in it up there. - And that's very bad. If you had not regulated that, but had only made the reservation in case your gloomy scenarios became reality that this would lead to exploitation and scandals, you could still have intervened later with a law. But for the sake of caution, you are already using, suffocating creativity, customer proximity and proximity to life – and that is a pity.

However, trade workers are added to those women who work in the hotel and inn, health and care sectors, in the teaching and service sectors of personal care and cleaning. They all work on Saturday, in the morning and in the afternoon: that is, until 1 p.m. or from 1 p.m. So we have almost reached the situation – if we follow media reports and what is being said here again and again, we can see that everything is dominated by location considerations, the race for competitive advantages and the demands that sound like wild deregulation. One gets the impression that Austria should only be brought to life in that country by the unblemished market orientation. However, I must contradict this because it is rather true that in many social areas we have to safeguard and protect the quality of life already achieved. In the specific case, we must ensure that the quality of life of the traded workers, for whom this means a restriction of leisure time, but also a restriction of family time, does not suffer from the economic benefits of extending opening time. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] Apart from disputes, the regulation of times is a highly sensitive matter and has a sensitive impact on quality of life. It is therefore foreseen that for those who work Saturday afternoon, from 1 p.m. on, a whole work-free Saturday must be followed. This is a small but very important change, as these changes are mainly at the expense of women. I've already said it: 70 percent of trade workers are women, women with families, women with children, but also single women who are facing appropriate problems on Saturday afternoon work. Because – this question must be allowed –: Where to with the children in the absence of childcare facilities and the changing social structures? You can apply: The child has a father!, but single, single mothers usually have no partner at hand. In addition: What do you do if your father is in business and working on Saturday? Of course, I am aware that society, which has changed leisure and consumer behaviour, should therefore come to a more flexible structure of working time, not least – I also agree – given the international competition and the safeguarding of Austria's economic location. But we should not lose sight of one thing either: The working time offensive currently running under the term "flexibilization" is nothing but the desire to let work on Saturdays, Sundays, holidays and at night. This means nothing but work around the clock and the adaptation of man to the machine. – That’s a family-anti-family, that’s an anti-child, that’s what we need to be aware of! [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] The result will be: Families and partnerships will be taken in the root. The resolution process will accelerate rather than bring about a stabilisation effect on the partnerships. It is very easy – and this is always happening – to conjure up the meaning of the family and the functioning of the family life, but it is also necessary to implement it, because in reality we make it increasingly difficult for families. You have to put values into practice, not just talk about it. I would like to point out the employment rate of women: More than half of the women with children are employed. Single women are double affected by the lack of childcare facilities and the pay gap between men and women for the same work done. It is then, of course, tempting to get out of work, with this overload due to lack of childcare facilities, poor working conditions, anti-family working hours, the income differences and the sole competence of the woman for family and educational work to give man and children – as it is formulated – a decent home. – That’s how it is said: to give a decent home. This allows the ironic reversing of the fact that women in work are offered an disorderly home. It must be clear to us that the group of salesmen is affected by the new regulation we are now considering, and that is a typical women's profession, a typical non-carriage profession. Health problems are added, especially in shopping centres and wholesale markets: only artificial light, train air, air conditioning, large concentration requirements over long periods of time – think of the supermarket cashiers –, constant time pressure, almost no breaks, permanent customer commitment. And only half of all employees on the weekend are free on two related days a week. For all these reasons, it is particularly important to ensure a sufficient regeneration phase in the form of two connected days off a week. I would like to take advantage of this opportunity and point out the importance of childcare facilities, the importance of farm kindergartens in connection with this question. These institutions make sense in particular if we want to take note of the problems that Saturday afternoon work and Saturday work entail for women and mothers and families not only, but also about the framework conditions. Companies where no works council exists are taken into account in the draft. In establishments without works councils, a written individual agreement may lay down that two Saturdays may be worked within a period of four weeks if the remaining two Saturdays are free of work. Some 37 400 enterprises are affected by this – small businesses with one to four employees. For example, 68 000 people in Austria are employed. Ladies and gentlemen! The role of politics, the role of labour rights, but above all the role of trade unions – with the representatives and the representation of workers in recent times constantly disqualified – are presented as being like a backlog to the rapid developments of the economy, as if the economy were the only modern one – and all others are lagging behind and lagging behind. However, the forthcoming modification of the opening hours will in any case, whatever the general terms and conditions, bring an extension of the opening hours to Saturdays. This is a step for which there is a lot of talk: Location considerations, apprehension of the flow of purchasing power, tourism; but also a step against which there is a lot of talk: family situation, quality of life; but also a step that needs to fit into the whole of society and that does not have to be achieved through which there are no benefits, which for others bring a minus of quality of life, free time and family life. The proposed regulation should protect against such development.

I always have a problem in medical care: It is ultimately supply-determined, it is not demand-determined. This is something which tends to be at increasing costs, because it is not a market. This does not mean, however, that it is not possible to bring together two providers, in this case private or public hospitals, fruitfully as a cost-controlling element. I would really ask you to think carefully about it in this direction, if this is dealt with in the Committee and to take up the positive in this proposal. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e10921	Ladies and gentlemen! I do not want to engage in the ideological debate, which form of childcare is the optimal. The fact is – and this has already been stressed by my previous speakers – that the mother-in-law needs social security. Mrs Haller, I am not at all so inclined to see positive elements in your proposal, but the problem at present is that, according to my typology, we have five different groups of social security for day mothers. We have the normal mother-in-law, who is insured under social and labour law, we have the minorly employed mother-in-law, who is only more insured under labour law, we have the mother-in-law with a contract of work, which, because they are neither over the low-quality limit, are nevertheless partially social insurance, and we have the mother-in-law under the social security limit for work contracts, which are neither under labour law nor under social law. Finally, we still have the group of day mothers employed by private individuals, in which there is no legal regulation at all. So we have five different categories of day mothers. There is hardly a professional group which can be found in as different forms of social and labour security as the mother-in-law. And if it were not the mother-in-laws – although the day-fathers are also called in parentheses – we would probably not have – I dare to claim – these different forms. There have already been several strong efforts to ensure that employment and social security are finally safeguarded. But because they are mostly women, they are allowed to do so in this country. This is the real scandal we should be discussing at this point: there are still five different groups. And I have the problem with your request, namely that a new one would be added to these five groups. We would then have a sixth group, which is somewhere in between, which, of course, tends to, after the other areas would not disappear from social and labour security, result in the day-mothers and day-fathers being probably no longer fixed. And that can't be the meaning of a regulation. If we had only the employed and the unemployed mother-in-laws without social legislation, then your request would make sense, then I would be very much in favour of us discussing this request not only seriously at first reading but also at a second reading here in this House. However, as long as we have five categories of day mothers in this situation, nothing changes, add only a sixth group with this model. And that can't be the sense of exercise.

I believe that such a policy would not only be appropriate for moral or otherwise reasons, but it does not, above all, correspond to the long-term security interests of Austria. Of course, these negotiations will not go fast. Nothing to get before the turn of the millennium. I also believe that the European Union as the primary project is pursuing monetary union. And as long as monetary union and the euro do not drive on solid rails, there will not be much to be done in this regard. Apart from that, the negotiations will also take a longer time for another reason, because the enlargement of the East will not be possible at all if the current regulations are simply maintained, especially in the area of regional funds, because the countries of Eastern Europe have such a low level of social products that they cannot achieve such transfers, such as Portugal, Ireland or Greece today. The fact that about 50 percent of its social product could be obtained from transfers from the EU in the future is excluded. The absorption capacity of Romania, but also of the other countries, is completely out of date with such transfers. No economy in the world could stand that. In other words: These regulations must be redesigned anyway. The regional funds and EU assistance to the poorer countries need to be redesigned. However, this also means that the current figures on the cost of joining the Eastern European countries are overestimated, as the current arrangements are not transferable. But until then we should not arouse the fear of people – neither in Austria nor in the European Union; Anxiety is always a bad advice – but rather a reasonable balance of interests between Western Europe and so-called Eastern Europe. Finally, I would like to say: However, an illusion should not be given to the government parties, namely that monetary union will solve the employment problem or the growth problem on its own. I do not think that will be the case, but monetary union will have a neutral impact on employment or growth policy. However, this also means that parallel employment policies have no time at international level, i.e. at EU level or at Austrian level. These must not wait – starting with the famous offensives in education, with apprentices, with technology and so on. All this doesn't have time. We can't wait for that. Everything else would be an illusion and a bad service to Austria. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e14837	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! In fact, it should be assumed that anyone who has sworn his oath to the Constitution – and in addition to the Members here in the House; including the members of the government – is interested in complying with the Constitution, but above all also that the laws that exist in our country are in accordance with the Constitution. I have this optimism that people are concerned about it, even though there are numerous counter-examples, especially in comparisons from the recent past. I am thinking, for example, of how this House - at least the Members of the Governments - acted here in terms of retroactive laws, although it was known that this violates a principle of our rule of law, namely the principle of trust and faith. I am thinking of how provisions in the Civil Service Act were simply decided by a constitutional majority only because they knew that they violate the principles of our Constitution. This has nothing to do with further development of the Constitution, but it has deliberately been eliminated from the control of the Constitutional Court because one knew that a provision would violate principles. It is already a reflection of the representatives of the government parties that, in every constitutional objection, they do not deal with how to make a provision more constitutional, but rather with the means of the two-thirds majority, simply by making a constitutional provision and thus avoiding the control of the Constitutional Court. Despite this painful experience in this House, I still have optimism that there must be some who want to have a constitutional state. I am not at all talking about the fact that we are now faced with plans which are going to go back to this Constitution. In fact, it is a fact that constitutionally determined bodies are decomposed, degraded and instead there is competence in bodies which do not have constitutional anchorage, such as the municipal government, such as the national conference, such as the city council. My request is to see the dimension of the state. Because what I have now mentioned in comparisons were rules of play that are within the system. It is bad enough if these rules do not comply with the constitutional principles, but it really becomes dangerous if the rules of the game that lead to the installation of the system no longer comply with the constitutional principles, because then it is so that we create a product that means that there is no critical question within this body, so that then the resistance must come from outside or comes. ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e14919	(Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Even if I very much appreciate your pathos and your constitutional plea, I wouldn't be like plurality with the right to vote. The Kärntner electoral right certainly controls a very strong fragmentation or, if you want to, Italian relations. A Carinthian Landtagsmandate corresponds to only about one percent of the votes cast or even less. In this way, each small group could have a certain right to a mandate. It must be said that until the 1970s, in concrete terms until 1974, Carinthia was a single constituency. The division into four constituencies was made only after the Constitutional Court had granted an election challenge. Therefore, there can be no talk of an extreme reduction of the constituencies, of a change of electoral system to a majority vote, as your request says. The ten percent of votes in favour of a mandate are also too high. In fact, the obstacle is about seven percent, and I must admit that it is very high. In my opinion, however, it should be assumed that there should be some acceptance on the ground of such a small number of elections. A strong ground mandate therefore also has a certain right. 8,000 to 9 000 votes in a constituency with 120,000 electorates seem to me to be not so much, given that there should be some local anchorage to the population. In this way, the high level of access is not so much a problem of the Constitution, but above all a problem because the Greens and the Liberals in Carinthia are relatively weak. But this is more a political problem than a constitutional one. The fact that the high level of entry – that is a fact – is prohibitive, as you put it in your request – by the way, an expression that I associate more with alcohol – by preventing potential voters from voting for small parties is, above all, a bad insult to your voters. They assume that they do not choose you because of your programme or because of your people, but only because of the power-political fact that you are potentially able to reach a mandate – which is not a priority at all. For so stupid, you should not think your voters. Whether a challenge against the Carinthian country elections would lead to a re-election, I cannot judge here because a correct election itself is not a reason for a re-election, in any case, the allocation of the mandates could be such a cause. So although I understand your basic concern – and I would like to stress this once again – I believe that this proposal for a deadline has been formally introduced at the wrong level. I believe that the decision on the Carinthian Landtagswahlverordnung has to be taken by the Carinthian Landtagslegislator, and I do not regard it as the task of the Federal Legislator or the Federal Government to interfere in the autonomy of the country of Carinthia. The question of the Kärntner electoral right is to decide and answer the country itself. Therefore, we will not give our consent to this request.

On the other hand, without them there is no outer property, whatever kind of property it is, to be enjoyed. Ladies and gentlemen! Nine-tenths of our luck! After all, a very large share. The question now is: What are these nine tenths of our happiness worth? The question is also: How can we find in our health system the balance between the understandable demand for the highest quality at all levels and the necessary funds? We must, I believe, create a system which points to a clear distribution of competence, which has a transparent cost distribution and includes future-oriented structures. Minister! You would have the chance of this historic reform! Where, please, are the clear rules of competence? – They vary between federalism and centralism. Negotiations between the Federal Government and countries are being postponed, stagnating, such as the Working Time Act, which should be on the agenda today and here. Minister! They have introduced a new financing system. Bravo, Minister! Well done! But the meaning of this financing system should have been to counteract hospital fatigue. It should have been that the necessary services are being carried out in the hospitals, but that the necessary services are not transferred to the extramural space. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] Minister! However, you take your own measures ad absurdly, because you know very well that social security schemes give only one covert amount to the national funds through their acceptance of contributions. They also know that social insurance now gives their patients to the hospitals and does not want to get out of the hospitals for the simple reason, because you only want to pay more of these covered amounts to the hospitals. There are letters from the social insurance companies that pre-operative benefits should now be made in the hospitals and no longer outside the hospitals. There are also letters from social insurance companies which clearly say that MRCT investigations should be transferred into the hospitals and not from the hospitals. Today, with great amazement, I read in the magazine "NEWS" that you propose a massive destruction of cash doctors. Mrs Minister, please bring light into the dark of your thoughts! That's where the cat bites in the tail. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] Minister! Where's the vision? Dr. Leiner: That's not true!]] And the discussion is inflamed by the word "responsibility". [[Abg. Dr. Leiner: That's not true!]] I'll prove it to you. - What's wrong with that? It should finally be stopped with patients at risk of working hours of up to 100 hours per week for hospital doctors. These arrangements are now finally requested by the EU directives of Austria. However, for the sake of self-interest, some federal states, especially those who have few specialist doctors, vehemently oppose these human laws on working time. Since yesterday, there should have been an agreement, but this agreement, as far as I know, is an absolute victory of the countries if there is this agreement at all. Guggenberger: This must be a more liberal!]] Minister! Against the background of the decision of the AZG, you have given the countries a political sugar, namely the willingness to call! What is the current § 8 in the KAG? – The medical service must be set up in such a way as to ensure, at any time, emergency medical care by a specialist from certain subjects present in the hospital and a willingness to call the special subjects in question. – That is to say, the EU’s law on working time is now practically undermined by the call. [[Abg. Dr. Leiner: Why?]] Currently, 600 doctors are missing for a law-compliant operation. By the end of the transitional period, as provided for in the Law on Working Time, 1 200 doctors would have to be recruited more. Now the Minister says that this is only a framework and it is in the discretion of the countries to set up higher standards. But reality is: The plan is aimed at savings. The reality is that, through the covert amounts of social security funds, the countries have only more very certain amounts at their disposal, that hospital costs will increase, but that less will be paid into social security schemes. That is, the sheer will continue to disperse. And where will the countries save? – Of course, in the case of staff costs, which account for 60 percent of the total hospital costs. Ladies and gentlemen! The declaration of assurance by the countries that they will maintain the standard is indeed a joke! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] It's really a joke, written on a guesthouse bill. Handwrittenly, Bishop Landesrat wrote his guarantee that he would maintain the standard. That's ridiculous! [[Abg. Dr. Leiner: Handshake is enough!]] Mr Commissioner, don't be angry with me, what do you mean by handshake and legal durability? That's just ridiculous. He's not supposed to sell us for stupid. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] [[Abg. Dr. Leiner: A word is enough!]] What are the exact consequences of this willingness to call for doctors and patients? Point 1: A specialist in standard hospitals is the minimum standard. This can lead to very grotesque situations that I do not want to address more closely now. Point 2: The first care of the stationary patients and the emergency patients, i.e. the first treatment, is performed by the gymnastician. The gymnist is known to be a doctoral physician without professional qualifications. That means that he is not entitled to self-examination of his profession. This is not a qualification of the gymnastics, it is the reality. I know because I was a gymnastic myself. Now, however, Mrs. Minister is trying to bend this law. And now, imagine: On the day – if there is enough staff, if the entire health care staff is present and if the specialists are there – the gymnastician must not be self-employed, the day he is what he is, the apprentice who learns. [[Abg. Dr. Pumberger: Just like the sorcerer!]] But at night he may suddenly, if he is allowed and he has the appropriate knowledge, do everything. During the night, he may suddenly provide the patient with hospital or outpatient emergency care completely independently, and only after that must he call the specialist. He can do this at night, even though he cannot do it in much better conditions during the day. That's inconvenient! That's negligent! This is just not sustainable by liability! And this is a massive loss of quality for the patient! Do you know what Dr. Schlögel says about the gymnastics representative of Austria? – He says: The colleagues are scared! - Sure, of course they're scared! They fear that for example, their superior will say: You can do that at night! You can call me if you need me! But then they are faced with the decision and may not know what to do in acute situations. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] Ladies and gentlemen! There are already very clear judgments. There is a judgment of the Supreme Court on the liability of the gymnastics doctor. A gymnastician in training for anaesthesiology has made the following: A ten-year-old child has been sent to the hospital for acute osteoarthritis, and the operating gymnist has just thought he could already, decided to anaesthetic this child. There has been an incident. As a result, the child was unconscious in the hospital for several weeks, but rehabilitation was only partial, resulting in the disability of this child now. This gymnastics doctor and the hospital carrier have been sentenced in both the first and the second instances. – But despite these judgments, this law is now being passed. Let us consider the whole from the patient’s point of view, not from the doctor’s point of view – I just want to tell you this example so that you know how it actually works: The following event occurred a year ago in Oberpulendorf: A young man was beaten down in a discotheque and then taken to the competent hospital. There the official gymnastics has been squeaked out. The patient has bleeding from three places, out of his eyes and out of his nose. X-rays were then made by this man, but the gymnastician was inconclusive what he was supposed to do with these X-rays. He said he'd actually have to spit out the callable ophthalmologist. But then he meant he didn't beep him out, he'd pick up these bad patients on Saturday, on Monday the ophthalmologist would come and he'd look at him. But the patient did not stay in this hospital, but went to another hospital. There it turned out that he had skull fractures, an open skull break with the withdrawal of brain fluid, a monocle hematoma, and more. Ladies and gentlemen! Gymnasticians are trainees for whom we have the responsibility. It is young doctors we cannot demand that at night they suddenly be able to do something that we do not believe in them in the day. This law is therefore not liable and not possible. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] Ladies and gentlemen! But let us take a look at this law, if one makes such a bad regulation, from the financial side: What would make these schemes cheaper? – There are two types of savings: The first would be that the conversion of the attendance service would be cheaper into a reputation. But that's only very marginal, because the moment the caller really goes to the hospital and works there, the payment is practically the same. This can easily be seen, for example, from the tariffs of the country of Upper Austria. The second type would be the future cost savings for the introduction of the AZG. If the AZG were to be introduced in the original form, then there would be a savings potential of 280 million schilling – there are calculations from the medical chamber. Well, 280 million are relatively much money, but we need to see this in relation to the total cost of doctors of 5 billion schilling. I would now like to make an amendment which represents the situation of the gymnastics. ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e18156	Mr President. High house! Ladies and gentlemen! Today's plenary debate on the novel of the Hospital Law is, I hope, the conclusion of the long, intense and very violent discussion over the past few weeks, which has, above all, aroused public awareness of the call. I do not want to repeat here all the arguments that I believe are in favour of the proposal, nor do I address the many counter-arguments. It has already been said by both sides. Even though I am convinced of the correctness of our path, I respect and respect the ideas that have been brought in by the opponents of our solution. On the substance of the controversy one can see that the actual discussion in its core goes far beyond the subject of callability. Ladies and gentlemen! The Austrian health care system is at the beginning of a phase of important changes that will be much wider and more incisive than the questions discussed here today. An analysis of the situation of our health care system and a look at developments in other industrialized countries shows that on the one hand, we will deal very intensively with the question of costs and therefore with the financing of supply and on the other hand with the issue of quality of services in the coming years. The problems which are now being solved are largely due to the successes of medicine and social policy over the last few decades. The extension of life expectancy, the shift of the epidemiological spectrum from acute infectious diseases to chronic degenerative diseases, and the almost incomprehensible rapid development of increasingly recent groundbreaking procedures in medicine ask us how we can continue to ensure the financing of the supply. Ladies and gentlemen! When answering this question, we must not be afraid of the accompanying discussions before we get to grips with this issue and a solution to the problem. It is the most essential and irrevocable priority to do everything in order to continue to enable optimal medical care for all people in that country. We must not allow the situation to arise that people are excluded from necessary medical care. However, changes are necessary in order to achieve this objective and thus to maintain the foundations of our health system. The developments that I described earlier and the budgetary scarcity of public budgets force us over the next few years to make far-reaching structural reforms. In principle, we have two options: the rationalisation of services and thus the exclusion of persons or groups of persons from necessary medical care and rationalization, i.e. the search for ways to make the best use of the available resources. For me it is undoubtedly clear that the solution can only be in the second way, in rationalization, in the more efficient use of the scarce means. The findings of research in the field of health economics confirm the reports of many doctors and health managers that a considerable part of the services already provided could be covered and saved without causing a deterioration in patient care. This issue must in future receive our full attention. In addition, the question of prevention and preventive medicine. We know that almost 45 percent of all deaths today go to the account of diseases, their causes in the behaviour and way of life of those affected. Smoking, unhealthy nutrition and lack of physical activity are not the only but the essential causes. To prevent this potentially avoidable "death before time" should be one of our main concerns. In addition, we have the question of the importance of stationary care in relation to the care provided by the established doctors. We know that in Austria, compared with other countries, much more services are provided in the hospital. We will have to ask ourselves how we can find a better distribution between these two levels of supply. These and similar questions are discussed. It will be mine and all our task in the next few years to solve it. Ladies and gentlemen! One thing is very important: The reforms of the financing and innovation of the Hospital Law, which we are implementing, must be seen as part of an overall approach to addressing the structural problems of the health system. What happens today is only the first steps on a long path. And we all have to go this way together – these are the representatives of the doctors, the care professions, the federal government, countries and communities. Only together can we best solve the problems. It is my concern to involve all those involved in the discussion, and I urge everyone to cooperate with us. The only prerequisite for this is the agreement on the principle that it is about ensuring optimum medical care for all in the long term. I would like to see joint solutions. I do not want to assume that the common path cannot be taken. Refusal or closing the eyes does not solve the problems. Nevertheless, the necessary measures will have to be put in place, and to these top health policy objectives I stand. – Thank you, ladies and gentlemen.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-11-29-020-XX-NRSITZ-00049_d7e18836	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Today, we are again discussing a resolution on feed bans for animal and bone meal from low hygiene plants. We have also recently discussed this issue here. I have already mentioned the lack of sterilisation security of some TKV plants within the EU. I have pointed out that plants that work without pressure and with dry heat are not really suitable to kill BSE pathogens and other heat-resistant germs, especially spore producers. Furthermore, I have pointed out that such inadequate installations are still in operation within the EU today and therefore contaminated products from such plants could also come to us in Austria. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! What fatal consequences of insufficiently heated and insufficient sterilized feed can be seen at the BSE epidemic in Great Britain and at negative effects across Europe. As recently in an expert hearing, our TKV systems – unlike these defective systems – have a high hygiene standard. This standard, in conjunction with the prohibition of feeding animal and bone meal to ruminants, is the reason that BSE and other animal diseases in Austria were thank God until now no problem. It's not just about BSE. I would remind you that swine fever has recently caused a damage in Germany of around 2 billion D-Mark and that in southern countries the foot-and-mouth disease is now back on. Therefore, in order to avoid such risks, a request for general feeding ban on animal and bone meal was originally submitted by Mr Firlinger. After expert discussions and knowing the high hygiene standard of the Austrian plants, a amendment was tabled by me, according to which the feeding ban should only apply to products from low hygiene plants where the danger to our pets and therefore also to humans cannot be excluded. As a result, a five-party application was made. This was sent to the plenary session and was scheduled to take place on 31 December. It was decided on October. However, since it cannot be what must not be said that one agrees to an oppositional request, although only in the Committee, the request made by Mr Firlinger was referred back to the Committee on Health. Mr Firlinger then withdrew his request – and rightly so. In order to ensure that this five-party motion, which would have been approved but which could still be decided, I quickly tabled a motion for a resolution to establish a basis for this five-party agreement. That is why I was surprised at the Committee on Health when the two government parties tabled the original five-party proposal and wanted to know nothing more about a joint request. Then the already grotesque situation became even grander: Both the opposition, i.e. the liberals, liberals and the Greens, as well as the government parties – apart from each other – have tabled a word-identical request, on which it was then voted separately. While the opposition parties, since both applications were identical, naturally agreed to both requests, within seconds SPÖ and ÖVP have changed their opinion like a Chameleon. You first rejected the opposition request to accept the word-like government request a few seconds later. Ladies and gentlemen! There is no better example of the government's exclusion policy from the opposition at all! My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen of the government parties! You haven't learned anything from the elections, as I think. They are not about physical policy, but only about measuring and limiting the opposition, especially the liberals. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen of red and black! However, you do not solve any problems, and the electorate, as I believe, will take account of such a policy.
